## EU Competition Watch: New rules for technology transfer agreements (and why finance people should care)

**What happened:** On **17 April 2026**, the Commission adopted **PI_COM:C(2026)2477**, a **Commission Regulation** setting out how **Article 101(3) TFEU** applies to **categories of technology transfer agreements**. In plain terms: it clarifies when certain technology licensing and related arrangements can be treated as compatible with EU competition law—provided they meet specified conditions.

**Why it matters:** Technology transfer is the quiet engine behind a lot of EU innovation activity: licensing IP, co-developing know-how, and structuring rights around patents, software, and technical processes. These deals sit at the intersection of **innovation policy** and **capital markets** because they shape (a) who can scale, (b) how quickly new products reach market, and (c) whether investors can underwrite predictable commercialisation pathways.

For policy and finance readers, the key point is that **competition-law certainty reduces deal friction**. When companies know the boundaries of “safe” agreement structures, they can negotiate faster, price risk more accurately, and avoid costly legal uncertainty that can delay funding rounds or procurement decisions.

---

### What the regulation is trying to do (without the legal fog)

Article 101(1) TFEU generally prohibits anti-competitive agreements. But **Article 101(3)** provides a route for agreements that produce efficiencies—such as promoting technical progress—while still leaving enough competition in place.

This regulation is essentially a **framework for categories of technology transfer agreements**: it tells the market how to apply the “efficiency” exception in a structured way. That matters because technology transfer agreements can easily contain clauses that—depending on design and market power—might raise competition concerns (for example, restrictions on use, field-of-use limitations, or exclusivity arrangements).

By setting out the application of Article 101(3) to **categories**, the Commission is aiming to:
- **reduce uncertainty** for contracting parties,
- **encourage pro-competitive licensing practices**, and
- **avoid over-deterrence** where legitimate innovation collaboration gets treated as risky by default.

---

### Programme/instrument links: none in the text provided
The excerpt you supplied contains **only the regulation metadata and title**, without any mention of specific EU funding instruments (e.g., **EDF, EDIP, InvestEU, EIB/EIF, SAFE, CMU**). So this summary focuses strictly on the competition-law development and its downstream effects on innovation and financing.

---

### € amounts, timelines, eligibility, financing changes
From the provided text, there are **no specific € amounts**, **no explicit financing changes**, and **no eligibility criteria** described beyond the regulation’s scope (technology transfer agreements) and its adoption date (**17 Apr 2026**).

However, the **timeline signal** is still meaningful: this is a **2026** Commission Regulation, so it should be treated as a current, operative reference point for parties structuring or revising technology licensing arrangements.

---

## Implications: startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

### 1) Startups and SMEs: fewer “legal tax” surprises
For smaller innovators, technology transfer agreements are often the difference between:
- a prototype that stays a prototype, and
- a licensing or collaboration deal that funds scale-up.

Competition-law uncertainty can be disproportionately expensive for SMEs because legal review and negotiation time can consume scarce runway. A clearer “how Article 101(3) applies” framework can:
- make licensing terms easier to negotiate,
- improve the bankability of IP-driven revenue models, and
- reduce the risk that investors discount deals due to competition-law ambiguity.

**Practical takeaway:** if you’re an SME licensing core IP (or taking licences to build products), ensure your agreements are aligned with the regulation’s category logic—especially around exclusivity, restrictions, and field-of-use.

### 2) Primes and technology integrators: deal structuring becomes more predictable
Defence and dual-use primes (and large tech integrators) often rely on layered IP arrangements: background IP licences, foreground IP allocation, and sub-licensing rights across supply chains.

A regulation that clarifies the application of Article 101(3) can help primes:
- standardise contract templates,
- reduce compliance overhead across multiple jurisdictions,
- and avoid renegotiations triggered by competition-law concerns.

**Practical takeaway:** primes should treat this as a prompt to review standard licensing clauses—particularly where exclusivity or restrictions are used to manage investment risk.

### 3) Regulators: more consistent enforcement signals
For regulators and competition authorities, category-based guidance supports consistency. That matters because technology transfer is a cross-cutting area: competition policy interacts with industrial policy, R&D collaboration, and procurement.

**Practical takeaway:** expect more coherent enforcement expectations around technology licensing structures, which should improve the quality of compliance and reduce “case-by-case” uncertainty.

## EU finance & innovation watch: codifying agricultural economic accounts (COM(2026) 159) — the unglamorous rule that keeps markets honest

If you’ve ever wondered why EU policy debates can sound like they’re arguing about the same numbers in different languages, this one’s for you. On **17 April 2026**, the Commission published an **amended proposal** for a **Regulation on the economic accounts for agriculture in the Union**, presented as **codification** (COM(2026) 159). In plain terms: it tidies up the legal text governing how the EU records and reports agriculture’s economic performance.

### 1) What happened and why it matters

The Commission’s move is a **codification** exercise: it consolidates existing legal provisions into a single, clearer instrument, typically without changing the underlying substance. That matters because agricultural statistics and accounting frameworks are a backbone input to:

- **policy design** (e.g., how support is targeted and evaluated),
- **budget and programme monitoring** (including how outcomes are measured),
- **risk assessment and forecasting** (for both public authorities and market participants),
- and **comparability across Member States** (so that “EU-wide” really means EU-wide, not “a patchwork of national interpretations”).

Even though codification rarely makes headlines, it can reduce friction for regulators and analysts by making the rules easier to apply and cite. For finance audiences, the key point is that **better-defined accounting rules improve the reliability of the data pipeline** feeding economic indicators used in decision-making.

### 2) Programme/instrument links (only where present)

This particular item is focused on **economic accounts for agriculture** and does not mention specific innovation/defence/capital-market instruments in the provided text. As a result, there are **no direct references** here to **EDF, EDIP, InvestEU, EIB/EIF, SAFE, or CMU**.

That’s not a gap in your watchlist—just a reminder that not every Commission publication is a “funding announcement”. Some are the plumbing.

### 3) € amounts, timelines, eligibility/financing changes

From the information provided, there are **no € amounts** and **no explicit financing/eligibility changes**. The only concrete timeline element is the **publication date**:

- **17 Apr 2026** (SOURCE_DATE)

Because the document is described as **codification**, the expectation is that it **reorganises** the legal text rather than introducing new funding mechanics or eligibility criteria.

### 4) Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

At first glance, a regulation on agricultural economic accounts may seem distant from startups, primes, and capital markets. But it still has practical implications—especially for firms operating in agri-food supply chains, agri-tech, and data-driven services.

**For startups/SMEs (including agri-tech and analytics):**
- **Data consistency is a competitive advantage.** When the EU’s accounting framework is clear and stable, companies building reporting tools, benchmarking platforms, or compliance analytics benefit from fewer interpretive mismatches.
- **Downstream funding and evaluation become more predictable.** Even if this regulation doesn’t directly allocate money, it can influence how programmes are assessed and how performance metrics are interpreted—indirectly affecting where opportunities land.

**For primes and larger contractors:**
- **Reporting and auditability matter.** Primes that work with public authorities (or that supply data for public reporting) rely on consistent statistical definitions. Codification can reduce legal ambiguity, which in turn can reduce compliance overhead.

**For regulators and public bodies:**
- **Lower legal friction.** Codification makes it easier to apply and reference the rules, which can improve implementation consistency across Member States.
- **Better comparability supports oversight.** Reliable economic accounts help regulators monitor sector performance and the effectiveness of interventions.

**For capital markets observers:**
- While this is not a capital markets regulation, it supports the broader ecosystem of **economic transparency**. Investors and analysts often use official statistics to benchmark sector health and macro exposure. Clear accounting rules help reduce “model risk” that comes from inconsistent inputs.

## EU battery EVs: countervailing duties land — a signal to markets, not just a tariff

**What happened?**  
The Commission has adopted **Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2754** (CELEX:02024R2754-20260211), imposing a **definitive countervailing duty** on imports into the EU of **new battery electric vehicles (BEVs) designed for the transport of persons** that **originate in the People’s Republic of China**. The measure is **dated 29 October 2024** and is reflected in the consolidated CELEX entry dated **11 February 2026**.

**Why it matters (the “connect the dots” bit):**  
This is not just a trade-policy headline. It’s a **capital-markets and industrial strategy** move with knock-on effects for:

- **EU automakers and their supply chains**: definitive duties change the landed cost of Chinese BEVs, which can shift demand, production planning and pricing power across the EU value chain.
- **Financing conditions for the transition**: when import competition changes abruptly, so do revenue forecasts—affecting credit risk, equity valuations and the appetite of lenders/investors for downstream manufacturing and component suppliers.
- **Regulatory expectations for “level playing field”**: the EU is effectively saying that **subsidy-related cost advantages** are not treated as a temporary market anomaly. They trigger **lasting** trade remedies (“definitive” is the key word).

In short: this is a **policy lever that feeds into industrial competitiveness**, and it will likely influence how investors price the next phase of EU EV capacity build-out.

Bron: `eur-lex.europa.eu` · Datum: 2026-04-17T05:23:26.259453+00:00  
Programma: Other/NA  
## EPPO on trial: the Court clarifies who can review what (and when) — and why it matters for effective justice

**What happened?**  
On **16 April 2026**, the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU) delivered **Judgment in Case C‑328/24 P**: **Constantin Mincu Pătrașcu Brâncuși v European Public Prosecutor’s Office (EPPO)**. The dispute centres on **Regulation (EU) 2017/1939** and, crucially, **how judicial review of EPPO procedural acts works** when those acts are intended to produce **legal effects vis‑à‑vis third parties**.

In short: the Court addresses the **admissibility** of a challenge (including a **plea of illegality**) and confirms the **limits of the General Court’s jurisdiction** in this context. The judgment also revisits the constitutional architecture: **exclusive CJEU jurisdiction** to review the legality of EU acts, and the relationship between **national courts** and the EU judiciary when EPPO actions are litigated.

**Why it matters (especially for policy/finance readers):**  
For financial markets and innovation ecosystems, the EPPO is not “just another regulator”. It is the EU’s cross-border enforcement engine for **EU financial interests**—the kind of enforcement that can affect **funding flows, due diligence, governance, and litigation risk** for corporates, primes, and SMEs alike.

This judgment is therefore less about day-to-day enforcement tactics and more about **procedural certainty**: *which court can hear what*, *how challenges can be framed*, and *what routes remain open* when EPPO procedural acts are contested. That is the difference between predictable legal pathways and costly jurisdictional detours.

---

## The legal knot the Court untied

The case turns on **Article 42(1) of Regulation (EU) 2017/1939**, which concerns **procedural acts of the EPPO intended to produce legal effects vis‑à‑vis third parties**. The judgment discusses **judicial review carried out by national courts** and how that interacts with EU-level legality review.

Key themes flagged in the case summary include:

- **Plea of illegality and admissibility**: whether and how a party can challenge the legality of relevant EPPO-related acts through procedural litigation.
- **Exclusive jurisdiction of the CJEU**: the Court reiterates that review of the legality of **EU acts** sits with the **CJEU**, not the General Court, in the way the Treaties structure it.
- **Article 86(2) and (3) TFEU**: the judgment references the Treaty framework governing the EPPO’s operation and the judicial review architecture.
- **No derogation authorised in Article 263 TFEU**: the Court indicates that the EPPO’s specific procedural review rules cannot create a derogation from the Treaty’s allocation of competence for legality review.
- **Article 47 of the Charter** (effective judicial protection): the Court addresses whether the procedural design undermines the right to effective judicial protection—and the summary indicates **no infringement** was found on that point.
- **Lack of jurisdiction of the General Court**: the case summary explicitly states that the General Court lacked jurisdiction.

**So what?**  
1) **Litigation strategy becomes clearer**: parties challenging EPPO procedural acts must align with the **Treaty-based court competence** rules—otherwise they risk inadmissibility or jurisdictional dead ends.  
2) **Regulatory predictability improves**: for firms operating across borders, knowing the correct judicial route reduces uncertainty in compliance, governance decisions, and dispute planning.

## Carbon allowances meet carbon tax: the Court clarifies how “free” emissions credits interact with national CO₂ taxes

**What happened?**  
On **16 April 2026**, the Court of Justice (Second Chamber) delivered **Judgment in Case C‑519/24**: *Nitrogénművek Vegyipari Zrt. v Nemzeti Adó- és Vámhivatal Fellebbviteli Igazgatósága* (CELEX: **62024CJ0519**). The case is a **preliminary ruling** on **EU emissions trading** rules—specifically **Directive 2003/87/EC**—and the **transitional rules for harmonised free allocation** under **Article 10a**.

At the heart of the dispute: **Hungarian national legislation imposed a tax on CO₂ emissions** on operators that receive a **significant quantity of allowances allocated free of charge**. The question for the Court was essentially how far Member States can go with **national carbon taxation** when an operator is already benefiting from **free allocation** under the ETS framework.

**Why it matters (and why you should care even if you’re not a “carbon nerd”)**  
For EU industry, the ETS is not just a compliance mechanism—it’s also a **pricing and investment signal**. Free allocation (especially during transitional periods) is designed to address **competitiveness and carbon leakage** concerns. But if a Member State can layer a **CO₂ tax** on top of that free allocation in a way that undermines the ETS design, you risk distorting the intended balance between:

- **EU-level harmonisation** (how free allocation is supposed to work), and  
- **national fiscal autonomy** (how Member States tax emissions).

This judgment matters because it affects the **effective cost of carbon** for ETS participants and can influence **investment planning**, **pricing of industrial inputs**, and **how firms structure production and hedging strategies**.

---

## Programme/instrument links (what’s in scope here)
This case is anchored in **Directive 2003/87/EC** (the EU ETS directive), and it turns on **Article 10a** (transitional rules for harmonised free allocation). No other EU funding or capital-market instruments are mentioned in the provided text.

---

## What the Court was asked to interpret (the “mechanics”)
The reference concerns:

- **ETS trading system rules**, including **transitional rules for harmonised free allocation**; and  
- whether **national legislation imposing a CO₂ emissions tax** on operators receiving **significant free allowances** is compatible with the ETS directive—particularly **Article 10a**.

In other words, the Court is being asked to police the boundary between **EU ETS design** and **national tax measures** that may interact with ETS benefits.

---

- **Judgment date:** **16 April 2026**  
- **Case reference:** **C‑519/24**

## Implications: startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators (yes, even beyond heavy industry)
Even though ETS participants are often large industrial emitters, the downstream effects can reach the broader ecosystem:

### 1) Startups and SMEs (especially industrial tech and supply-chain players)
- **Cost pass-through becomes clearer (or at least more predictable):** If the Court’s interpretation limits or conditions how national CO₂ taxes can apply to beneficiaries of free allocation, it can reduce uncertainty in **unit economics** for firms in energy-intensive supply chains.
- **Funding and diligence:** Investors increasingly look at **regulatory risk** as part of underwriting. A clearer ETS/tax interaction can improve the quality of **regulatory risk modelling** for industrial startups, especially those with emissions-intensive operations or those selling into ETS-regulated customers.

### 2) Primes and contractors (defence-adjacent and industrial primes included)
- **Contracting and pricing clauses:** Primes that rely on industrial inputs subject to ETS and national CO₂ measures may need to revisit **indexation** and **carbon cost adjustment** clauses. If national taxes are constrained or reinterpreted, the “carbon line item” in bids may shift.
- **Procurement compliance:** Where procurement depends on predictable carbon costs (e.g., long-term supply contracts), legal clarity helps reduce disputes and renegotiations.

### 3) Regulators and tax authorities
- **Harmonisation pressure:** The case is a reminder that national measures interacting with ETS free allocation must respect the directive’s structure. Regulators may need to review whether their national tax design inadvertently conflicts with the ETS transitional allocation logic.
- **Administrative consistency:** Expect follow-on guidance or adjustments in how authorities treat operators receiving free allowances—particularly where “significant quantity” triggers tax treatment.

1) **Free allocation isn’t a shield for national fiscal policy—unless the directive says it is.** The Court’s job is to determine whether the ETS framework leaves room for CO₂ taxes that effectively neutralise or distort the intended transitional balance.

## Thuiskopie, paid streaming and the “fair compensation” puzzle: what C‑496/24 means for EU IP policy and the money behind it

On 16 April 2026, the Court of Justice of the EU (Second Chamber) delivered its judgment in **C‑496/24**: *Stichting Onderhandelingen Thuiskopie vergoeding and Stichting de Thuiskopie v HP Nederland BV and Others* (CELEX:62024CJ0496). The case is a **preliminary ruling** on how several provisions of **Directive 2001/29/EC** (the Copyright Directive) fit together—especially around **private copying**, **fair compensation**, and the treatment of **paid streaming services** that enable **offline copies**.

### 1) What happened—and why it matters

At the heart of the dispute is the interaction between:

- the **reproduction right** (Article 2),
- the **right of communication to the public** (Article 3(1)),
- the **private copying exception** (Article 5(2)(b)),
- and the rules on **fair compensation** (Article 5(2)(b) and Article 5(5)).

The Court was asked to consider a scenario involving a **paid streaming service** that allows users to make an **offline streaming copy**. In plain terms: when a user pays for streaming and then stores a copy for offline listening/viewing, does that offline copy fall under the private copying exception—and if so, what does “fair compensation” require?

Why this matters for policy and finance readers: **private copying levies and compensation mechanisms are not just legal trivia**. They can affect:
- the economics of consumer devices and platforms,
- the revenue models of rights-holders and collecting societies,
- and the cost base for manufacturers and distributors across the EU.

Even when the case is framed as copyright law, it has **real cashflow implications**—and those cashflows can influence investment decisions, pricing strategies, and market entry for both incumbents and newer digital services.

### 2) Programme/instrument links (only where present)

This specific judgment summary does **not** mention any EU funding or capital-market instruments (e.g., **EDF, EDIP, InvestEU, EIB/EIF, SAFE, CMU**). So there’s nothing to connect here on that front.

### 3) € amounts, timelines, and eligibility/financing changes

No **€ amounts** or specific **financing changes** are included in the text provided to me. What we *do* have is a clear **timeline**:

- **Judgment date:** **16 April 2026**
- **Source publication date (CELEX page):** **17 April 2026**

On **eligibility**, the case is about the scope and conditions of the **private copying exception** and the **fair compensation** requirement—particularly in the context of **paid streaming** and **offline copies**. The key practical question is whether the legal system treats offline copies made via a paid service as “private copying” that triggers compensation, or whether the existence of a paid service changes the analysis under Article 5(5).

### 4) Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

**For startups and SMEs (especially in streaming, content tech, and device ecosystems):**
- If offline copies made through paid services are treated as triggering private copying compensation, platforms may face **additional compliance and cost considerations** (e.g., licensing/levy arrangements, reporting, and contract structures).
- If the Court narrows or clarifies when fair compensation applies, it could reduce uncertainty for new entrants—**but only if the ruling translates into predictable practice** by collecting societies and rights-holders.

**For “primes” and larger incumbents (platforms, device manufacturers, rights-holders):**
- The decision is likely to influence how compensation is calculated and collected. That can affect:
  - negotiations with collecting societies,
  - pass-through pricing to consumers,
  - and risk management for cross-border product launches.

**For regulators and courts:**
- The judgment provides interpretive guidance on Directive 2001/29/EC—meaning national authorities and courts will need to align their approach to:
  - what counts as a relevant act under Article 2/3,
  - when Article 5(2)(b) applies,
  - and how Article 5(5) limits the exception.
- In practice, regulators will be watching for consistency: **private copying systems can’t be “one size fits all” when business models evolve** (streaming with offline functionality is the modern twist).

### 5) Two “so what” insights (smart, not hype)

1) **Offline copies are the new fault line.** The Court’s focus on paid streaming and offline copies signals that EU copyright exceptions are being stress-tested by product features, not just by traditional “copying” behaviour.

2) **Fair compensation is a market design tool, not a footnote.** How the Court interprets Article 5(2)(b) and Article 5(5) will shape the incentives and costs across the value chain—from platforms to device makers—affecting investment and pricing more than many observers expect.

## EU competition rules get a technology-transfer tune-up (and it matters for innovation deals)

**What happened:** The Commission has published a **Commission Staff Working Document (SWD:2026:114:FIN)**—an **impact assessment** accompanying two competition-law updates:  
1) a **Commission Regulation** on the application of **Article 101(3) TFEU** to **categories of technology transfer agreements**, and  
2) **Communication/Guidelines** on how **Article 101** applies to **technology transfer agreements**.

**Why it matters:** Technology transfer is the quiet engine behind a lot of EU innovation—licensing IP, cross-licensing, know-how sharing, and structured collaborations between universities, startups, corporates, and defence/dual-use primes. But competition-law uncertainty can chill deal-making: parties may over-comply, delay negotiations, or avoid certain clauses altogether. This package aims to clarify how the EU competition rules apply to these agreements, and—crucially—how they can still benefit from the **efficiency exception** in **Article 101(3)** when conditions are met.

### 1) What’s changing, and why it matters for innovation and capital markets
The document is explicitly framed as an **impact assessment** for a **Commission Regulation** and **Guidelines** focused on **technology transfer agreements**. In practice, this is about reducing legal ambiguity around whether common deal structures are compatible with EU competition law.

For finance and innovation ecosystems, the knock-on effects are straightforward:

- **More predictable contracting**: If parties can price and structure licensing and collaboration with clearer guardrails, transactions become easier to close and easier to finance.
- **Lower “compliance friction”**: Uncertainty is costly—lawyer time, longer diligence cycles, and deal redesigns. Clearer rules can shorten time-to-sign.
- **Better matching of risk and return**: Investors (including those backing deep-tech and dual-use innovation) care about enforceable rights and predictable governance. Competition-law clarity can support that.

**So what (insight #1):** In innovation markets, legal uncertainty often behaves like a tax on speed. Clarifying Article 101 application for technology transfer agreements can reduce that tax—helping deals move from “promising prototype” to “commercially usable IP”.

## EU competition law gets a tech-transfer tune-up: clearer rules for how innovation moves (and how firms share it)

**What happened and why it matters**  
On **17 April 2026**, the Commission adopted **PI_COM:C(2026)2482**, a **Communication setting out Guidelines on the application of Article 101 TFEU to technology transfer agreements**. In plain English: the Commission is clarifying how EU competition law applies when companies license, assign, or otherwise share technology—especially where those deals sit close to the boundary between legitimate collaboration and anti-competitive coordination.

Why this matters for innovation and defence-adjacent markets is simple: **technology transfer is the bloodstream of R&D**. It’s how universities, research performers, primes, and specialist SMEs turn know-how into deployable products—whether for industrial scale-up, dual-use capabilities, or defence supply chains. Article 101 is the legal “traffic light” for agreements that could restrict competition (for example, by fixing prices, limiting output, or carving up markets). These guidelines aim to make that traffic light **more predictable**—so firms can structure deals that are competition-compliant without slowing down innovation.

**What’s the practical shift?**  
The Communication is a **guidance document**, not a new law. But guidance can be a big deal in finance and policy terms because it affects:
- **Deal structuring** (licence scope, exclusivity, field-of-use, territory, grant-backs, and cross-licensing)
- **Risk pricing** (how much legal/compliance cost and uncertainty is baked into transactions)
- **Regulatory expectations** (what the Commission is likely to view as pro-competitive versus problematic)

For policy watchers, the key point is that the Commission is focusing on **technology transfer agreements specifically**, rather than treating them as generic commercial contracts. That signals a recognition that innovation markets often involve **complementary assets** and **standard-setting dynamics**, where the same clause can be benign in one context and risky in another.

So, the “change” here is **procedural and interpretative**: firms should expect the Commission’s approach to Article 101 in this domain to be more clearly signposted.

**Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators**  

### 1) Startups and SMEs: less fog, faster licensing  
For smaller innovators, the biggest friction in technology licensing is often **uncertainty**—not just about whether a deal is allowed, but about how regulators will interpret it later. Clearer guidance can help SMEs:
- negotiate licences with **less fear of inadvertent Article 101 exposure**
- structure **non-exclusive** or **field-limited** licences more confidently
- justify why certain restrictions (e.g., to protect investment incentives) are **pro-competitive**

That said, SMEs still need to be careful: technology transfer deals can involve **market power** and **dependency**. Guidance doesn’t remove the need for legal review—it reduces the guesswork.

### 2) Primes and consortium leaders: compliance becomes part of contracting design  
For primes and large integrators, technology transfer agreements are often embedded in broader collaboration frameworks (R&D partnerships, supply arrangements, and cross-licensing). The guidelines are likely to influence:
- **contract templates** and internal approval processes
- how exclusivity and grant-backs are drafted
- how cross-licensing is handled when multiple parties contribute complementary IP

In defence and dual-use ecosystems, where collaboration is common but competition concerns are sensitive, predictable competition-law treatment can reduce delays in contracting—particularly when multiple suppliers need access to enabling technologies.

### 3) Regulators: more consistent enforcement expectations  
For regulators and competition authorities, sector-specific guidance supports **consistency**. It can also help national authorities align their assessments with the Commission’s view, reducing the risk of divergent interpretations across Member States.

**So what? Two smart insights**  
1) **Innovation speed depends on legal certainty.** Even when funding is available, projects stall if licensing and collaboration agreements are too risky to sign quickly. These guidelines are a “friction reducer” for the deal pipeline.  
2) **Technology transfer is not automatically pro-competitive—or anti-competitive.** The Commission’s move to issue targeted guidance underscores that the same clause (exclusivity, restrictions, cross-licensing) can be either a legitimate incentive mechanism or an anti-competitive lock-in, depending on context.

In plain terms: the EU is refining how competition law treats deals where one party licenses or transfers technology to another—think software, industrial know-how, patents, and platform-related IP. Article 101(1) can catch restrictive arrangements, but **Article 101(3)** allows exemptions where agreements generate efficiencies (e.g., innovation, consumer benefits) and don’t eliminate competition.

Why this matters for innovation and capital markets is that **technology transfer is the plumbing of scaling**. Licensing and IP-sharing are often the bridge between R&D and revenue—especially for **startups and SMEs** that may not have the balance sheet to manufacture, distribute, or vertically integrate. If the competition-law “safe space” is unclear, parties either over-comply (slower deals, higher legal costs) or under-comply (higher enforcement risk). Either way, the cost of capital rises—quietly, but measurably.

Bron: `eur-lex.europa.eu` · Datum: 2026-04-17T05:22:18.600904+00:00  
Programma: Other/NA  
## Court clarifies “inside information” when public procurement decisions meet market abuse rules

**What happened (and why it matters)**  
On **16 April 2026**, the Court of Justice (Fourth Chamber) delivered **Judgment in Case C‑229/24 (TK and OP v Riksåklagaren)**. The case is a **preliminary ruling** on how to interpret **Regulation (EU) No 596/2014 (Market Abuse Regulation, MAR)**—specifically the concept of **“inside information”** under **Article 7(1)(a)** and the conditions for **public disclosure** under **Article 17**.

At the heart of the dispute: a **contracting authority decided not to award a public contract** to a tenderer, and there was an **early sale of shares** by that tenderer. The question for the Court was essentially whether that procurement-related development (and/or any related communications) amounted to **inside information**, and what counts as **“public disclosure”** for MAR purposes—particularly in light of **Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/1055** (notably **Article 2**, on means for public disclosure).

For **capital markets participants**, this matters because MAR is the EU’s backbone for deterring trading on non-public, price-sensitive facts. If the boundary between “public” and “non-public” information is unclear, compliance becomes guesswork—and enforcement risk rises. This judgment tightens the interpretive framework for situations where **public-sector procurement** intersects with **issuer information** and **trading decisions**.

---

## The legal mechanics: MAR’s “inside information” and “public disclosure” tests

The Court’s ruling focuses on two linked MAR concepts:

1. **Inside information (Article 7(1)(a))**  
   The Court examines the **criteria** for whether information is “inside information”. The case turns on the idea that not all information connected to an issuer is automatically inside information; the key is whether it is **public or not**, and whether it is **precise and price-sensitive** in the MAR sense.

2. **Public disclosure by an issuer (Article 17)**  
   MAR provides a route for issuers to make information public in a way that changes its status. The Court addresses what “public disclosure” means in practice, including the **means** used to disclose.

3. **Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/1055, Article 2**  
   The judgment explicitly references the implementing rules on **means for public disclosure**. In other words, the Court is not only interpreting MAR’s substance; it is also clarifying the procedural “how”.

---

## What likely happened in practice (the scenario the Court had to map)

The fact pattern, as framed in the reference, is straightforward:

- A **contracting authority** made a **decision not to award** a **public contract** to a **tenderer**.
- The tenderer then engaged in an **early sale of its shares**.
- The prosecution/authority argued that the tenderer’s trading reflected knowledge of **inside information**.
- The legal question was whether the procurement decision (or its implications) was **public** (or made public in a MAR-compliant way), and therefore whether the information was **inside** at the time of trading.

This is a classic “grey zone” scenario: procurement processes are public-sector and often documented, but the **timing**, **access**, and **market visibility** of decisions can vary. MAR compliance depends not on whether something is “known somewhere”, but on whether it is **publicly available** in a manner that meets the regulation’s disclosure standards.

---

## Timelines and amounts: none stated, but the timing question is the point

The case summary provided does **not** specify:
- any **€ amounts**,
- any **funding instrument** amounts,
- or a **programme timeline**.

However, the **timeline** is implicit and central: the Court is concerned with the **moment** when information becomes **public** (or not) and how that affects whether trading is prohibited.

So, while there’s no headline funding figure to track, there is a compliance timeline to watch: **the trading window relative to disclosure**.

---

## Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

### For startups and SMEs (including defence-adjacent innovators)
Even if you’re not a “prime”, you can still be an issuer (or connected to one) and therefore caught by MAR. The practical takeaway is:

- **Procurement outcomes can be market-moving**. If a tenderer’s prospects change materially, the information may be price-sensitive.
- **“We told the contracting authority” is not the same as “we made it public under MAR.”** If disclosure is incomplete, delayed, or not in the required form, the information may remain inside.

For smaller issuers, the compliance burden can be heavy—so the judgment’s emphasis on **means of public disclosure** is particularly relevant. It suggests that firms should treat procurement decision milestones as **potential MAR triggers**, not just legal/procurement events.

### For primes and market-facing counterparties
Primes often sit in the middle of procurement ecosystems and capital markets. They should expect:

- **More scrutiny of trading and information flows**

## Ukraine Plan: Council approval lands—what it means for EU innovation, defence and capital markets

**What happened?**  
The Council adopted **Council Implementing Decision (EU) 2024/1447** approving the **assessment of the Ukraine Plan** (decision dated **14 May 2024**). The legal text is published under **CELEX:02024D1447-20260223**.

**Why it matters (the “connect the dots” bit):**  
For EU policy and finance watchers, this is one of those “quiet but consequential” steps. Approval of the assessment is not just administrative housekeeping: it is a **gatekeeping milestone** that affects whether and how the EU can proceed with the **implementation of the Ukraine Plan**—including the credibility of the plan in the eyes of EU institutions and, by extension, the confidence of investors and counterparties that rely on EU-backed frameworks.

In practice, these decisions tend to influence three things at once:

1. **Delivery discipline**: once the assessment is approved, the plan’s structure and milestones become the reference point for subsequent implementation and monitoring. That reduces uncertainty for actors trying to align projects, procurement pipelines and financing structures.
2. **Capital mobilisation**: when EU plans are assessed and endorsed through formal Council decisions, it can improve bankability—especially for projects that sit at the intersection of **defence-adjacent industrial capacity**, **innovation**, and **infrastructure**.
3. **Regulatory signalling**: regulators and procurement authorities can treat the plan as a stable policy anchor rather than a moving target, which matters for compliance planning and contracting.

## Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

Even without the article’s operational details, Council approval of an assessment typically has knock-on effects across the ecosystem:

### 1) Startups and SMEs: clearer demand signals, but still a procurement reality check  
- **Opportunity channel:** When a plan is formally assessed and approved, it tends to stabilise the policy environment in which contracting authorities and prime integrators plan capability build-outs. That can help SMEs that are trying to position dual-use or defence-relevant innovation offerings.
- **Reality check:** SMEs still face the usual barriers—**access to procurement**, **qualification**, and **working-capital constraints**. A plan approval can improve predictability, but it doesn’t automatically fix payment terms or reduce administrative load.

### 2) Primes: more bankable planning, fewer “moving target” risks  
- **Programme alignment:** Primes often structure consortia and supply chains around policy milestones. An approved assessment reduces the risk that downstream requirements shift after the fact.
- **Financing and risk allocation:** Even when EU money is not directly flowing to a prime, EU-endorsed plans can support counterparties’ risk assessments—particularly where projects require long lead times and complex compliance.

### 3) Regulators and public buyers: a stronger reference point for monitoring and implementation  
- **Compliance and reporting:** Regulators generally prefer stable, formally endorsed benchmarks. Council approval helps anchor monitoring expectations.
- **Procurement governance:** Contracting authorities can align procurement strategies to the plan’s assessed structure, which can reduce ad hoc decision-making.

## “So what” insights (smart, short, and not hype)

1. **Policy approval is a financing signal, not a funding cheque.** Even when the money mechanics aren’t visible in the excerpt, formal Council endorsement typically improves implementation credibility—useful for banks, investors, and industrial planners who hate uncertainty.
2. **The biggest winners are often the ones who can translate policy into contracts.** Startups and SMEs benefit most when they can plug into prime supply chains and procurement frameworks that follow from approved plans—so the operational interface matters as much as the headline decision.
